## Page 9

About the Author

Dr. Rashād Khalifa was born in the midst of the highly spiritual and mystical world of Egyptian Sufism. His spiritual teacher, ‘Abdul-Ḥalīm Khalifa, grand Shaykh of Tariqat Al-Rashād Al-Shāziliyya, is also his physical father. From his early childhood right through young manhood, the author was a constant companion of his father during his evangelistic missions across Egypt. He was also a constant participant in the nightly “Zikr” sessions led by his teacher/father. The climax of his Sufi indoctrination took place in Mecca (1391 AH; 1971 AD), where he was officially initiated into the chain of Sufi deputies. The first link in this chain is acknowledged by all Sufis to be the initiation of ‘Alī ibn Abī-Ṭālib by the Prophet Muḥammad (May God exalt him and bless him). From that initial indoctrination, the present schools of Sufism have evolved.

Dr. Khalifa belongs to the school of Sufism which advocates not only full participation in the worldly life, but also transforming the daily worldly affairs into a form of worship. This concept was first adopted by the great imam of Sufism ‘Alī ibn ‘Abdillah, better known as Abul-Ḥasan Al-Shāzily (593-656 AH; ca 1195-1257 AD). In keeping with this concept, Shaykh ‘Abdul-Ḥalīm Khalifa is a retired engineer who spent 40 years in the employ of the Egyptian government. His spiritual teacher, the famed Shaykh Salāma Al-Rāḍy (1284-1357 AH; 1867-1939 AD) also served the Egyptian government as an accountant. These two great Sufi leaders, and many

## Page 10

others who belong to Al-Shāzily’s school, were dedicated, efficient, workers at their jobs. The only complaint by their worldly “superiors” had to do with the crowds of students and dervishes who waited by the government buildings’ entrances every morning and afternoon.

The author of this book followed the footsteps of his spiritual teachers. After going to the Qur’an school commonly known in Egypt as “Kuttāb,” Dr. Khalifa attended the public primary and secondary schools of his home town, Tanṭā. He went to Cairo for his college education, and when he graduated from the University of ‘Ain Shams’ Faculty of Agriculture in 1957, he ranked the first of his graduating class. This qualified him for a scholarship to study in the USA. He attended the University of Arizona (1959-1961), where he obtained the M.Sc. degree, and the University of California, Riverside (1962-1964), where he earned the Ph.D. His major field of study was Plant Biochemistry. He worked for the University of California (1964-1966), the Egyptian government (1966-1968), and Monsanto Company (1968-1970). Since 1970, he has been a research scientist with the Food Biochemistry group at Anheuser-Busch, Inc. As a senior member of the Central Research Department, he is searching for new sources of foods and food ingredients as part of the universal effort to feed the world’s growing populations.

During the past ten years, Dr. Khalifa has been an active member of the Muslim Students Association of the United States and Canada, the Federation of Islamic

## Page 11

Associations, and the Muslim community of greater St. Louis. He founded the first Muslim Students Association at the University of Arizona in 1960, and participated in establishing the first Islamic Society of Riverside, California. He served as secretary for the Association of Muslim Scientists and Engineers in North America during 1972 and 1973. In May 1972, he founded Islamic Productions International, Inc., aiming at “delivering the Message” to the English-speaking world. By means of these activities, Dr. Khalifa is maintaining the modern Sufi traditions of his spiritual teachers ‘Abdul-Halīm Khalifa, Salāma Al-Rādy, and Abul-Hasan Al-Shāzily.

## Page 12

### Introduction

Many people find themselves today in a position similar to that of the prophet Abraham (peace be upon him) some 4,000 years ago: believing, but in need of reassurance. This is so especially in an age when science has been employed almost exclusively for materialistic and faith-shattering purposes. Such science has allegedly discovered a plethora of evidence “proving” that there is no God, no Revelation, no afterlife, and that therefore man and the world are but the result of purposeless chance and mindless evolution. When scientific substantiation appears to be overwhelmingly on the side of the materialists, atheists, and agnostics, it is not unusual for the rational man of faith to feel the need for reassurance.

Abraham asked God for proof of a certain disputed matter and God inquired: “Do you not then believe?” Abraham responded: “Yes, but I seek to reassure my heart.” (Qur’an 2:260) Further, this request was not rebuffed by the Almighty; rather, God provided Abraham with demonstrable evidence which made more firm the belief he possessed already.

The conclusions based on Dr. Khalifa’s astounding research provide similar faith-strengthening evidence. They make it evident that for all eternity no one will be able to meet the challenge to write a book like the Qur’an or to write so much as a sura (chapter) like its suras. Nothing as magnificent as the Qur’an exists in ancient history, and if any man or group of men could live for a thousand years with just one task: to create

## Page 13

a book like the Qur’an, it is unlikely even then that they would succeed. Instead, science confirms that the Qur’an is beyond human capacity to compose or duplicate.

God tells us in sura Al-Qiyāmat, verse 17, that the responsibility to compose the Qur’an and determine its method of recitation is His alone. Now, for the first time, as a result of this painstaking research, the world can appreciate how impressive and intricate a matter composing the Qur’an was, and how balanced and beautiful a composition it is! Those who believe in the Qur’an will have their belief intensified with this proof of the Qur’an’s unique arrangement. Those not possessing such faith already will nevertheless have objective, tangible evidence which demonstrates to them the miracle of the Qur’an.

The Qur’an is God’s word, His Revelation to all mankind, the Book *par excellence* for guiding and developing the human family into a progressive unit. It behooves all mankind to pay attention to this Guidance.

Sulayman Shahid Mufassir

## Page 14

### Preface

Arabic-speaking Muslims, or non-Muslims, who read the various English translations of Qur’an become immediately aware of the frequent deficiencies, inaccuracies, and inconsistencies in those translations. The need for an accurate English rendering of the meanings of Qur’an is clearly evident to every Muslim who is sufficiently familiar with the Arabic language. This is especially true when we realize that the Qur’an has never been translated into English previously by a Muslim Arab. This fact alone explains the lack of “spirit” of the Qur’anic language in the various English translations.

With deep humility and full realization of the awesomeness of responsibility in translating God’s message, in 1368 AH (1968) the task of rendering the meanings of Qur’an into English was undertaken. The project proceeded smoothly through the first seven verses, namely, sura Al-Fātiḥa (The Opener). Then it came to an abrupt halt. The first verse of the next sura, Al-Baqara, consists of three letters of the Arabic alphabet: Alif Lām Mīm (A.L.M.). To find a satisfactory tafseer (explanation) of these apparently meaningless letters, a number of the great classic tafseers were consulted. These included the immortal works of ibn Kathīr, Al-Bayḍāwī, Al-Alūsī, Fakhr Al-Rāzī, Al-Jalālain, Al-Suyūṭī’s “Al-Itqān Fī ‘Ulūm Al-Qur’an,” and Al-Baqillānī’s “I’jāz Al-Qur’an.” Some of these references offered a number of hypotheses dealing with the mysterious alphabets. The various hypotheses are reviewed in detail

## Page 15

in the text of this book. Some others, including Al-Jalālain, did not even attempt to explain these letters, stating that “only God knows the meaning of such letters.” The references which offered some explanation unanimously declared that their tafseers were purely speculative. No definite tafseer was found.

In relaxed determination not to pass up a single word without a reasonable understanding of its tafseer, research was launched to find out the significance of Alif Lām Mīm. Developments of the research, which took four years (1968-1972), are detailed in the text. Only guidance from God Almighty could lead to the unveiling of certain aspects of the “Qur’anic initials.” The final development stage of researching these letters required a count of all the alphabets within each sura of the Qur’an, with the deployment of electronic computers to analyze the numbers obtained. This book reports the marvelous results of this first computerized study of the Qur’an.

The indulgence of readers who are not familiar with Arabic is requested with regard to the Arabic writings in this book. The translated Qur’anic verses must always be accompanied by the Arabic text. This is to avoid the inadvertent re-translation of the English into Arabic which is different from the original.

Rashād Khalīfa

St. Louis, Missouri

Ramadān, 1393

October, 1973

Prologue

بِسْمِ اللَّهِ الرَّحْمَٰنِ الرَّحِيمِ
قَالُوا لَوْلَا أُنْزِلَ عَلَيْهِ آيَةٌ مِّن رَّبِّهِ ۖ قُلْ إِنَّ اللَّهَ يُضِلُّ مَنْ يَشَاءُ وَيَهْدِي إِلَيْهِ مَنْ أَنَابَ
وَاللَّهُ غَنِيٌّ حَمِيدٌ
قُلْ لَوْ كَانَ الْبَحْرُ مِدَادًا لِّكَلِمَاتِ رَبِّي لَنَفِدَ الْبَحْرُ قَبْلَ أَنْ تَنفَدَ كَلِمَاتُ رَبِّي وَلَوْ جِئْنَا بِمِثْلِهِ مَدَدًا

In the name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful. They said, “If only a miracle could come down to him from his lord?” Say (O Muhammad), “Miracles are manifested only by God. I am no more than a warner, clarifier.” Is it not enough of a miracle for them that We revealed to you this book which is being recited to them? Indeed, in it there is mercy and a message for those who believe. (29:50-51)

These verses from sura Al-'Ankabūt inform us that the Qur’an is Muhammad’s miracle. Many other verses throughout the Qur’an state that the Qur’an is a divine scripture whose composition is beyond the literary ability of any other being, be it human or otherwise. Thus, verse number 88 of sura Al-Isrā’ (No. 17) states, “If all the humans and all the jinns banded together to produce a Qur’an like this, they will surely fail, no matter how much assistance they lend one another.”

لَوْلَا نُزِّلَ عَلَيْهِ الْقُرْآنُ جُمْلَةً وَاحِدَةً فَإِذًا لَأَنتُمْ لَهَزِئُونَ

The Qur’an challenges anyone to produce a literary work that exceeds or equals the Qur’an’s standard of excellence (2:23, 10:38, 11:13, 52:34). But, it should be pointed out that the miraculous nature of Qur’an is by

## Page 18

no means limited to its literary composition. The multidimensional miracle of Qur’an has allowed every generation in the past 14 centuries to witness new miracles in the Qur’an. As the mysteries of the universe are unveiled by advancing science and technology, man discovers that the “new” information has been long established in the Qur’an. Examples of these discoveries include the earth’s rotation and movement: “When you look at the mountains, you would think that they are standing still. But they are moving swiftly, just as you see the movement of the clouds. Such is the skillful creation of God, who perfected everything. Surely, He is cognizant of anything you do.” (27:88)

وَتَرَى الْجِبَالَ تَحْسَبُهَا جَامِدَةً وَهِيَ تَمُرُّ خَفْقَةً كَالْعَبِيرِ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ فَعَلَهُ اللَّهُ ۖ وَمَنْ أَحْسَنُ مِنَ اللَّهِ قِصَاصًا

Long before it was discovered by man, the Qur’an had stated that the sun is a “source” of light, while the moon is a mere reflection thereof. This has been mentioned consistently in sura Yūnus (10:5): “It is He who rendered the sun a lamp, and the moon a light.”

وَجَعَلْنَا اللَّيْلَ وَالنَّهَارَ آيَتَيْنِ ۖ فَمَحَوْنَا آيَةَ اللَّيْلِ وَجَعَلْنَا آيَةَ النَّهَارِ مُبْصِرَةً

and in sura Al-Furqān (25:61): “Blessed is He who placed in the heaven constellations, and He placed in it a lamp and a lighted moon,”

جَعَلَ فِيهَا رَوْنَقًا وَقَمَرًا مُّبِينًا

and in sura Nūh (71:16): “And He rendered the moon in (the heaven) a light, and He rendered the sun a Lamp.

وَجَعَلْنَا اللَّيْلَ وَالنَّهَارَ آيَتَيْنِ ۖ فَمَحَوْنَا آيَةَ اللَّيْلِ وَجَعَلْنَا آيَةَ النَّهَارِ مُبْصِرَةً

## Page 19

Prior to the discovery that the Pharaohs’ bodies were preserved, the Qur’an indicated that the ancient Egyptians were endowed with the hitherto unknown science of mummification. They were given such knowledge in order to preserve the body of the anti-Moses Pharaoh, and set him up as an example for subsequent generations. This revelation is in sura Yūnus (10:92): “We will preserve your body to render you an example for subsequent generations.”

![Arabic Text](نَحْنُ نَمُوتُ وَنَحْيَا وَمَا مَاتَ مِن دَابَّةٍ إِلَّا بِإِذْنِ اللَّهِ)

The body of anti-Moses Pharaoh is currently on display at the Cairo Museum.

Equally miraculous is the total absence from the Qur’an of nonsense statements. The Qur’an was revealed in an era dominated by ignorance, superstition, and a multitude of absurd beliefs. For example, it was widely believed that the earth was carried on the horns of a giant bull. When the bull switched from one horn to the other, this caused earthquakes. Despite the predominance of this and similar beliefs, the Qur’an does not contain any statements disproven by modern science.

The miracles of previous messengers of God which are narrated in the Bible and the Qur’an were visually perceptible performances witnessed by a few contemporaries. For example, how many people actually saw the staff of Moses (peace be upon him) turn into a snake? How many people actually saw Jesus Christ (peace be upon him) revive the dead, and create birds from clay?

## Page 20

Since the Prophet Muḥammad (peace be upon him) is the final messenger, it is only logical that his miracle should be a perpetual one. (Of course, all miracles belong to God. It is in relative context only that we speak of the prophets’ miracles.) The Qur’an is a mental miracle which is continuously witnessed by those fortunate enough to gain access to it. [As mentioned in sura Al-An‘ām (6:25), sura Al-Isrā’ (17:46), sura Al-Kahf (18:57), and sura Al-Wāqi‘a (56:77-79), the Qur’an is not available to just anyone who reads it. One has to be sincere and impartial if the Qur’an is to be made available to him] Thus, the miracle of Muḥammad (peace be upon him) has been witnessed by his own generation; it is being witnessed by the present generation; and it will be witnessed by every subsequent generation till the Day of Judgement. A prerequisite for this perpetual witnessing of the Qur’an’s miracle is that it must be preserved. For this reason, God Almighty Himself vowed in sura Al-Ḥijr (15:9) to preserve His final message: “We have revealed this message, and, surely, We will preserve it.” إِنَّا نَحْنُ نَزَّلْنَا الذِّكْرَ وَإِنَّا لَهُ لَحَافِظُونَ

Of all divine scriptures, ONLY the Qur’an remains unaltered. Today, we can buy the Qur’an in Tokyo, Cairo, Washington, or Karachi, and find all these copies identical with each other, and identical with the Quran preached by Muhammad (peace be upon him) and recited by him as he led the prayers 14 centuries ago.

One of the unique features of the Qur’an is the occurrence at the beginning of certain suras of alphabets

## Page 21

which bear no apparent meanings. These alphabets, henceforth called “Qur’anic Initials,” are not known in any other literary work. The study of the Qur’anic initials which is reported in this book is another demonstration of the eternal nature of Muḥammad’s miracle, the Qur’an.

## Page 22

# Chapter 1

## Prerequisite Information About The Qur’an

To derive maximum benefit from this study, a review of some basic information about the Qur’an is required. Since we will be dealing with many words and alphabetic letters of the Arabic language, a convention for the pronunciation and transliteration of the Arabic alphabet is presented in Table 1.

The Qur’an is made up of 114 chapters (suras) of varied lengths. Each sura consists of a number of verses ranging from 3 [suras Al-'Asr (no. 103), Al-Kawthar (108), and Al-Nasr (110)] to 286 verses [sura Al-Baqara (no. 2)]. The location of revelation of each sura is designated as “Meccan” or “Medinan.” All suras revealed before Hijra (the Prophet’s migration from Mecca to Medina) are considered “Meccan,” while those revealed after Hijra are considered “Medinan,” regardless of the exact location of revelation. Recording of the Qur’anic suras follows a specific sequence which is different from the revelation order. Table 2 lists the suras according to their recording sequence. The chronological sequence, i.e., the revelation sequence is shown in Table 3. The location of revelation of each sura is shown in both tables.

## Page 30

There has always been one version of the Qur’an, recorded during the lifetime of the Prophet Muḥammad (peace be upon him). The slightest alteration or distortion of this one version renders it Qur’an no more. Since the Prophet was keenly aware of the distortions which afflicted previous scriptures, he instructed the writers of God’s revelation never to write anything from him except those Qur’anic revelations. He made it clear to them that this was but one precaution against the accidental injection of non-Qur’anic material into God’s message. The Prophet personally supervised the recording and organization of the Qur’an down to the individual verses within each sura. Zeid ibn Thābit, the foremost revelation writer and a constant companion of the Prophet, is reported by Al-Bukhāry and other sources of Ḥadīth as saying, “We used to be with the messenger of God (peace be upon him) when we wrote the Qur’an on sheets. Whenever a verse, or a number of verses, was revealed, the Prophet would say, ‘Put this verse here, and that verse there,’ exactly as (the angel) Gabriel had taught him, in accordance with the will of God Almighty.”

Evidence for the existence of the Qur’an, as we know it today, during the Prophet’s life is abundant. For instance, the classic references of Al-Hākim, Ibn Khuzeima, Ibn Majah, and Al-Bayhaqy, report that the Prophet said, “After the death of a believer, certain of his righteous deeds continue to yield credit for him: the knowledge he had taught to others, pious offspring whom he left behind, and the Qur’an inherited from him.” This

## Page 31

inherited Qur’an is understood to be in the physical form of a book, or in the intangible form of memory. Recitation of the Qur’an by the Prophet as he led the prayers is reported by numerous companions, and is shown to be identical to the Qur’an in existence today.

Al-Bukhāry gives the following account for the recording of the Qur’an: “It was ‘Umar ibn Al-Khaṭṭāb who became concerned about the possible loss of the Qur’an. In the battle of Yamāma, ‘Umar witnessed the falling of a number of martyrs who had the Qur’an committed to their memories. ‘Umar then alerted the Khalīfa Abū Bakr, first successor to the Prophet, and he too became concerned. ‘Umar and Abū Bakr called Zeid ibn Thābit, widely known as the Prophet’s secretary, and asked him to collect the Qur’an and write it down in one unit to serve as a permanent reference. About this assignment, Zeid said, ‘In view of the awesome responsibility entrusted to me, it would have been an easier task had the Khalīfa commanded me to move a mountain. I went around collecting the very original writings of Qur’an which had been transcribed on pieces of leather, wood, bones, and rocks, as well as from the memories of men. The collected materials were checked against each other for accuracy.’ After completing his assignment, Zeid handed the recorded Qur’an over to Abū Bakr. When Abū Bakr died, the second Khalīfa, ‘Umar, took charge of this Qur’an. When ‘Umar, died, his daughter Ḥafṣa, a widow of the Prophet, kept the original recorded Qur’an. During the reign of ‘Uthmān

## Page 32

ibn ‘Affān, one of his emissaries to Syria and Iraq, named Ḥuzeifa ibn Al-Yamān, came to him and informed him of serious variations in reciting the Qur’an by the Muslims of Syria and Iraq. Ḥuzeifa said to ‘Uthmān ‘O prince of the faithful, save this nation before they distort their scripture as the Christians and Jews have distorted theirs.’ ‘Uthman immediately sent for the Qur’an from Ḥafṣa, and ordered the reproduction thereof. Four of the original revelation writers were charged with the reproduction work. They were Zeid ibn Thābit, ‘Abdullah ibn Al-Zubair, Sa‘īd ibn Al-‘As and ‘Abdul-Raḥmān ibn Al-Hārith ibn Hishām. The reproduced copies were dispatched to Basra (Iraq), Kūfa (Iraq), Damascus (Syria), and Mecca. Other copies were kept in Medina.” These copies were further reproduced and they represent the originals for the Qur’an published today.

## Page 33

Chapter 2

# The Qur’anic Initials

Of the 114 suras in the Qur’an, 28 begin with one set of Qur’anic initials, and one sura is prefixed with two sets of Qur’anic initials. Each set of Qur’anic initials consists of a number of letters ranging between one and five. The different sets of Qur’anic initials and the suras where they occur are listed in Table 4. As shown in that table, there are 14 different sets of Qur’anic initials, and the number of letters which participate in the make-up of these initials is also 14. The 14 letters involved in the formation of Qur’anic initials are Alif (A), Hā (H), Rā (R), Sīn (S), Ṣād (Ṣ), Ṭā (Ṭ), ‘Ayn (‘A), Qāf (Q), Kāf (K), Lām (L), Mīm (M), Nūn (N), Hā (H), and Yā (Y). These letters are known in the Muslim literature, more specifically among the Sufi mystics, as “the illumination letters [حروف النور](https://example.com)”. The other half of the alphabet, the 14 letters not involved, are known as “the darkness letters [حروف الظلام](https://example.com)”. To explain the reasoning behind this nomenclature, we would have to go beyond the scope of this book.

The suras which contain Qur’anic initials are listed in their recorded sequence in Table 5. The same suras, listed in their chronological (revelation) sequence, are shown in Table 6. As we shall see later, both sequences are important in the final analysis of the Qur’anic initials.

## Page 37

Chapter 3

REVIEW OF THE LITERATURE

During the past 14 centuries, a number of theories were developed dealing with the possible significance of the Qur’anic initials. The most comprehensive reviews of these theories are those reported by Al-Suyūṭy’s famous reference, “Al-Itqān Fī ‘Ulūm Al-Qur’an,” and Al-Bayḍāwy in his well-known commentary on the Qur’an. There is an unanimous agreement among the Muslim scholars who studied the Qur’anic initials that these “mysterious” letters have something to do with the miraculous nature of the Qur’an. Most of those scholars considered the magnificent literary composition of the Qur’an, and the fact that it was revealed through an illiterate man (the Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him), to be the miracle of the Qur’an. Accordingly, it has been generally believed that the Qur’an’s frequent challenge for anyone to produce anything like it, is a challenge to compose a literary work as beautiful, precise, and linguistically perfect as the Qur’an.

The most predominant theories explaining the Qur’anic initials can be summarized as follows:

1. The Qur’anic initials represent a challenge for anyone to compose from these alphabets a literary work as awesome as the Qur’an.
2. The Qur’anic initials were placed at the beginning of certain suras to attract the listeners’ attention.

## Page 38

3. The Qur’anic initials are secret or symbolic names of God.

4. The Qur’anic initials are symbolic names of the suras where they occur.

5. The Qur’anic initials are secret or symbolic names of the Qur’an, similar to Al-Furqān and Al-Dhikr.

6. The Qur’anic initials are words in a foreign tongue Al-Bayḍāwy, however, dismissed this theory on the basis that the Qur’an repeatedly states that it was revealed in a “perfect Arabic tongue.”

7. Ibn ‘Abbās theorized that the letter Alif (A) in Alif Lām Mīm stands for Allāh (God), the Lām (L) stands for the angel Gabriel, and the Mīm (M) stands for Muḥammad. Thus, this set of Qur’anic initials informs us that the Qur’an came from God to the Prophet Muḥammad (peace be upon him), through the mediation of Gabriel.

8. Ibn ‘Abbās also combined three different sets of initials, namely, Alif Lām Rā (A.L.R.), Hā Mīm (H.M.), and Nūn (N), to spell out the word Al-Rahmān, one of God’s magnificent names.

9. The most recent and comprehensive study of the Qur’anic initials is that presented by ‘Abdullah Yūsuf ‘Alī in his Qur’anic translation. His study is reproduced here in its entirety:

APPENDIX I.

The Abbreviated Letters (Al-Muqatta‘āt)

Certain Sūras have certain initials prefixed to them, which are called the “Abbreviated Letters”. A number of conjectures have been made as to their meaning. Opinions are divided as to the exact meaning of each particular letter or combination of letters, but it is agreed that they have a mystic meaning.

## Page 39

Mystic meaning, not intelligible at first sight, is not inconsistent with the character of the Qur-án as a "plain book". The book of nature is also a plain book, but how few can fully understand it? Every one can get out of the Qur-án plain guidance for his life according to his capacity for spiritual understanding. As his capacity grows, so will his understanding grow. The whole Book is a Record for all time. It must necessarily contain truths that only gradually unfold themselves to humanity. Even parables and tales of mystic meaning employ symbolism. The plain man may find the symbolism helpful, as a soldier finds his National Flag helpful. But what proportion of British soldiers or citizens understands the full symbolism of the British Union Jack?

This is not a mystery of the same class as "mysteries" by which we are asked to believe against the dictates of reason. If we are asked to believe that one is three and three is one, we can give no intelligible meaning to the words. If we are asked to believe that certain initials have a meaning which will be understood in the fullness of time or of spiritual development, we are asked to draw upon Faith, but we are not asked to do any violence to our reason.

I shall try to discuss some of the probable meanings of any particular abbreviated letter or set of abbreviated letters on the first occasion on which it appears in the Qur-án. But it may be desirable here to take a general view of the facts of their occurrence to help us in appreciating the various views which are held about them.

There are 29 letters in the Arabic alphabet (counting *hamsa* and *alif* as two letters), and there are 29 Sūras which have abbreviated letters prefixed to them. One of these Sūras (S. xlii.) has two sets of abbreviated letters, but we need not count this Sūra twice. If we take the half of the alphabet, omitting the fraction, we get 14, and this is the number of letters which actually occur in the Muqatta'at.

The 14 letters, which occur in various combinations, are:

![Letters](letters_image)

The science of phonetics tells us that our vocal sounds arise from the expulsion of the air from the lungs, and the sounds are determined by the way in which the breath passes through the various organs of speech, e.g., the throat (guttural), or the various positions of the tongue to the middle or front of the palate or to the teeth, or the play of the lips. Everyone of these kinds of sounds is represented in these letters.

Let us now examine the combinations.

Three of these letters occur alone, prefixed each to only one Sūra. The letters and Sūras are:

S. xxxviii. ![Alif](alif)
S. l. ![Qaf](qaf)
S. lxviii. ![Kaf](kaf)

The combinations of two letters occur in ten Sūras as shown below. Three of them occur only once each, but the fourth ( ![Alif-Lam](alif_lam) ) occurs in seven consecutive Sūras.

S. xx. ![Alif-Lam](alif_lam)
S. xxvii. ![Alif-Lam](alif_lam)

S. xxxvi.

```

```

S. xli.
S. xlii.

S. xlvi.

Note that S. xlii. has a double combination of abbreviated letters, one of two followed by one of three. See under combinations of five.

There are three combinations of three letters each, occurring as follows in 1. Sūras:

```
سورة ii.
سورة iii.
سورة xxix.
سورة xxx.
سورة xxxi.
سورة xxxii.
سورة x.
سورة xi.
سورة xii.
سورة xiv.
سورة xv.
سورة xxvi.
سورة xxviii.
```

Combinations of four letters occur twice, each only once:

```
سورة vii.
سورة xiii.
```

```
عز
```

Note that the three preceding and the two following Sūras have the triple letters عز.

Finally there remain the combinations of five letters, each of which occurs once only, as follows:

```
سورة xix.
سورة xlii.
```

In S. xlii. the عز and عز are put in separate verses. From that point of view they may be considered two separate combinations. The first combination has already been listed under the group of two-letter combinations.

This arithmetical analysis brings certain facts into prominence I do not know how far they have a bearing on the inner meaning of the Muqatta'at.

The combinations of abbreviated letters that run in a series in consecutive Sūras is noticeable. For example, عز occurs in seven consecutive Sūras from xl. to xlvi. The combination عز occurs in six consecutive Sūras x. to xv., but in one of them (S. xiii.) it is modified to عز, connecting it with the عز series. The عز series covers 6 Sūras. It begins with S. ii. and S. iii., which are practically the beginning of the Qur-an, and ends with the four consecutive Sūras xxix. to xxxii. I call S. ii. and S. iii. practically the beginning of the Qur-an, because S. i. is considered a general introduction to the Qur-an, and the first Sipara is commonly known as عز, the first verse of S. ii. The combination عز is prefixed to S. xxvi. and S. xxviii., but the intervening S. xxvii. has the combination عز, which may be considered a syncopated form, or the three-letter combination عز may be considered an extended form of عز.

## Page 41

Again the question arises: Does the ف in طسم , خحى , and يس , stand for the same signification, or does it mean a different thing in each case? We may generalise and say that there are three series of six, and one series of three, and the others occur all singly.

We should logically look for a common factor in the Sūras bearing the same initials, and this factor should be different for Sūras bearing other initials. In all cases where the abbreviated letters occur, there is some mention of the Qur-án or the Book. The Itqán makes an exception in the case of three Sūras, 'Ankabūt (S. xxix), Rūm (S. xxx), and Nūn (S. lxviii). But a close perusal will show that these Sūras are no exceptions. In xxix. 27 we have a reference to the Book remaining in the family of Abraham, and later on we have a whole Section, (Rukū' No. 5), devoted to the Book, with special reference to the continuity of revelation in the previous Books and the Qur-án (xxix. 45-51). In xxx. 58 there is express mention of the Qur-án, and the whole argument of the Sūra leads up to the intimate relation between God's "Signs" in nature (xxx. 20-27) and His revelation in the Qur-án. In S. lxviii. the very first verse begins the theme with the Pen as the instrument of writing, exhorts Mustafá to stand forth boldly to proclaim the Message, and ends (lxviii. 52) with the declaration that it is a Message for all the worlds.

These are general considerations, which I have thought it most convenient to present in the form of an Appendix.

In his explanation of the Qur'anic initials Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād, 'Abdullah Yūsuf 'Ali said,

989 This is a combination of four Abbreviated Letters. For Abbreviated Letters generally, see Appendix I (at the end of Sūra II). The combination here includes the three letters A. L. M., which occurred at the beginning of Sūra II, and are discussed in n 25 to ii. 1.

The additional letter Ṣād occurs in combination here and in Sūra xix, and by itself at the beginning of S. xxxviii, and nowhere else. The factor common to S. vii, S. xix, and S. xxxviii, is that in each case the core of the Sūra consists in the stories (qisas) of the Prophets. In this Sūra we have the stories of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, Shu'aib, and Moses, leading up to Muḥammad, and in S. xxxviii the stories of David Solomon, and Job similarly lead up to Muḥammad, occupying three out of the five sections. Sūra xix consists almost entirely of such stories. Can we understand Ṣād to stand for qisas, of which it is the most characteristic letter? In this Sūra vii. we have also the spiritual history of mankind traced—the Beginning, the Middle, and the End, which, as explained in n. 25, might be represented symbolically by A. L. M. If so, this Sūra, dealing with the Beginning, Middle, and End of man's spiritual story, and illustrating it by the stories of the Prophets, might well be represented symbolically by the letters Alif, Lām, Mīm, Ṣād. But no one can be dogmatic about these symbols. We must accept them as symbols with a mystic meaning.

His explanation of the initials Alif Lām Rā was as follows:

As shown in Appendix I (Sipāra 3), the Abbreviated Letters are mystic symbols, about whose meaning there is no authoritative explanation. If the theory advanced in n. 25 to ii. 1 has any validity, and the present group A.L.R. is cognate to the group A.L.M., we have to consider and form some idea in our minds as to the probable meaning of the variation. We took A.L.M. to be a symbol of those Sūras that deal with the beginning, the middle, and the end of man's spiritual history—the origin, the present position, and the things to come in the Last Days (eschatology, to use a theological term). We took A.L. to stand as symbols of the first two, and M. of the last. In the present group of Sūras we find hardly any eschatological matter, and therefore we can understand the absence of M., the symbol standing for such matter. In its place comes R., which is phonetically allied to L. L. is produced by the impact of the tongue to the front of the palate, and R. to the middle of the palate. In

## Page 42

many languages the letters L. and R. are interchangeable; e.g., in Arabic, *al-Rahmān* becomes *ar-Rahmān*, and R. in imperfect enunciation becomes L., as in Chinese lallations. If L. is a symbol of present-day things looking to the future, we may take R. as a symbol of present-day things looking within, i.e., into the interior of the organization of the Ummat. And this symbolism fits in with the subject-matter of the Sūras in question. But no one should be dogmatic in speculation about mystic Symbols.

**The Alif Lām Mīm Rā at the beginning of sura Al-Ra‘ad (no. 13) was explained as follows:**

1798. For A.L.M., see ii. 1, n. 25. For A.L.R., see Introduction to S. x. For abbreviated Letters generally see Appendix I. Here there seems to be a combination of the groups A.L.M. and A.L.R. We consider here not only the beginning (A.), the middle (L.), and the end (M.), of man’s spiritual history, but also the immediate future of the interior of our organisation, such as it appeared to our Ummat towards the close of the Meccan period. But in trying to understand mystic symbolism, we must not be dogmatic. The befitting attitude is to say: God knows best.

**As for the letters Kāf Hā Yā‘ Ayn Ṣād of sura Maryam (no. 19), A. Y. ‘Ali said,**

2455 This is the only Sūra which begins with these five Abbreviated Letters. K. H. Y. ‘. Ṣ For Abbreviated Letters generally, see Appendix I, pp 118-120.

As stated in my note 25, such Letters are Symbols, of which the true meaning is known to God alone. We should not be dogmatic about any conjectures that we make. According to the interpretation of the last letter Ṣād, suggested in n 989 to vii. 1, I should be disposed to accept Ṣād with the meaning of Qisas, i.e., stories of the Prophets. The main figures referred to here are: Zakariya, Yabya, Maryam, ‘Isa, and Ibrāhim: the others are mentioned but incidentally. The strong letter in Zakariya is K; in Ibrāhim, H; in Yabya and perhaps Maryam, Y; and in ‘Isa, ‘A (Alif). Ḥ also comes in Hārūn (Aaron), and the Arabic Yā comes in all the names including Ismā‘īl and Idrīs.

I offer this suggestion with some diffidence. The suggestion of the Tafsīr Kabīr is that the letters stand for attributes of God: K. for Kāf (the One sufficient in Himself); H for Hādī (He who guides); Y for Yad (Hand as a symbol of Power or Authority Cf. alviii. 10, “The Hand of God is above their hands”); ‘A. for ‘Alīm (the All-Knowing); and Ṣ for Ṣadiq (The True One).

**And in his introduction to sura Tā Hā (Ṭ. H.), he offered the following explanation:**

The mystic letters Tā Hā are prefixed to this Sūra. What do they mean? The earliest tradition is that they denote a dialectical interjection meaning “O man!” If so, the title is particularly appropriate in two ways: (1) It was a direct and personal address to a man in a high state of excitement, tempted by his temper to do grievous wrong, but called by God’s Grace, as by a personal appeal, to face the realities, for God knew his inmost secret thoughts (xx. 7): the revelation was sent by God Most Gracious, out of His Grace and Mercy (xx. 5). (2) It takes up the story from the last Sūra, of man as a spiritual being and illustrates it in further details. It tells the story of Moses in the crisis of his life when he received God’s Commission and in his personal relations with his mother, and how he came to be brought up in the Pharaoh’s house, to learn all the wisdom of the Egyptians, for use in God’s service, and in his personal relations with Pharaoh, whom we take to be his adoptive father (xxviii. 9). It further tells the story of a fallen soul who misled the Israelites into idolatry, and recalls how man’s Arch-enemy Satan caused his fall. Prayer and praise are necessary to man to cure his spiritual blindness and enable him to appreciate God’s revelation.

## Page 43

The three letters **Ṭā Sīn Mīm**, which occur in suras Al-Shu‘arā’ (no. 26) and Al-Qasas (no. 28) were explained as follows:

3137. This is a combination of three Abbreviated Letters, as to which, generally, see Appendix I, printed at the end of S. ii. This particular combination occurs here and at the head of S. xxviii, while the intervening Sūra xxvii has it in the syncopated form Ṭā Sīn. None of the explanatory conjectures which I have seen carries conviction to me. If the letters stand for Ṭūr-i-Sīnīn (Mount Sinai) and Mūsā (Moses), whose story fills a large part of this Sūra, why is the letter Mīm omitted in S. xxvii, where the same meaning would apply? There is, however, one fact to which I should like to draw attention. There are eleven sections in this Sūra, and eight of them end with the word Rāḥīm (with the final Mīm). The three exceptions are sections 2 and 3, and section 11. But sections 2 and 3 are part of the story of Moses, which is completed in section 4, and that ends with “Rāḥīm”. The main argument in section 11 ends at verse 217, which ends with Rāḥīm. We can say that the whole Sūra is based on a refrain ending in the word “Rāḥīm”. Whether this has any bearing on our present enquiry I cannot say. My own position is that where we have material, we should pursue our researches, but we should never be dogmatic in such matters, as some Mysteries can never be solved by mere research.

‘Ali’s explanation of Yā Sīn (Y.S.) was as follows:

3943. Some Commentators take Yā to be the vocative particle, and Sīn to be the abbreviation of insān. Sīn being the only “Firm Letter” in the word. In that case it would be an address to man. “O man!” But “man” in this connection is understood to mean the leader of man, the noblest of mankind, Muhammad the Prophet of God. For this Sūra deals mainly with the holy Prophet and his Message. But no dogmatic assertion can be made about the Abbreviated Letters, for which see Appendix I, following S. ii. Yā Sīn is usually treated as a mystic title of the holy Prophet.

The explanation of the letter Sād which initials sura Sād (no. 38) was as follows:

4146. Ṣād is a letter of the Arabic alphabet. It is used here as an Abbreviated Letter, for which see Appendix I (at the end of Sūra ii). See also the second para. of n. 989 to vii. I for this particular letter. No dogmatism is permissible in trying to interpret Abbreviated Letters. But it is suggested that it may stand for Qisas (“Stories”), in which the dominant consonant is ṣ. For this Sūra is concerned mainly with the stories of David and Solomon as illustrative of the relative positions of spiritual and worldly power. Sale’s note: “it may stand for Solomon”: is a real howler: for in Arabic the letter Ṣād does not occur at all in the name of Solomon.

For the Qur’anic initials Hā Mīm (H.M.), A. Yūṣuf ‘Ali offered the following explanation:

We now begin a series of seven Sūras (xl-xlvi) to which are affixed the Abbreviated Letters Hā-Mīm. Chronologically they all belong to the same period, the later Meccan Period, and they immediately follow the last Sūra in time. About the Abbreviated Letters generally, see Appendix I at the end of S. ii. As to the precise meaning of Hā-Mīm no authoritative explanation is available. If Mīm here has a signification similar to Mīm in A.L.M. (see n. 23 to ii), it means the End of things, the Last Day, and all these Sūras direct our special attention to that. Hā, the emphatic guttural, in contrast with the softer breathing of Alif, may be meant to suggest that the Beginning (see n. 25 to ii. 1, last paragraph) is only for the End, the Present for the Future, and to emphasize the eschatological element in Faith. But this is mere conjecture, and should be taken for no more than it is worth.

## Page 44

Another suggestion worthy of consideration is that **Hā** stands for **Ḥayy**, and **Mīm** for **Qaiyūm**. These are two attributes of God, meaning, (1) the Living, and (2) the Self-Subsisting, Eternal. The one points to Life and Revelation, and the other to the Hereafter and Eternity; and both these matters are specially dealt with in the seven **Hā-Mīm** Sūras. The first letter of **Ḥayy** (**Hā**) is appropriate for life, and the last letter of **Qaiyūm** is appropriate for the Last Days, the **Ma‘ād**, the Hereafter. Again, this is mere conjecture, and should not be taken for more than it is worth.

The initial **Qāf** of sura **Qāf** (No. 50) was explained in this manner:

4939. This is an Abbreviated Letter. For Abbreviated Letters generally, see Appendix I (pp. 118-120). This particular Abbreviated Letter **Qāf** occurs only here as a single letter, and in combination at the beginning of S. xlii., where I was unable to explain the full combination. Here the **Qāf** is taken by several Commentators to represent **"Qadḥiya-l-amru"**, "the matter has been decreed," with reference to the eschatological trend of the Sūra. God knows best.

And finally, the letter **Nūn** of sura **Al-Qalam** (no. 68) was explained as follows:

5592. **Nūn** is an Abbreviated Letter: see Appendix I at the end of S. ii.

**Nūn** may mean a fish, or an ink-holder, or it may be just the Arabic letter of the alphabet, N. In the last case, it may refer to either or both of the other meanings. Note also that the Arabic rhyme in this Sūra ends in N. The reference to ink would be an appropriate link with the mystic Pen in verse 1. The reference to the fish would be appropriate with reference to the story of Jonah in verses 48-50. Jonah's title is "the Companion of the Fish" (Zun-Nūn, xxi. 87), as he was, in the story, swallowed by the Fish. The letter N could also symbolically represent Jonah in the Arabic form **Yūnus**, where the characteristic "Firm Letter" is N.

## Page 45

# Chapter 4

## DEVELOPMENT OF THIS RESEARCH

Several hypotheses concerning the Qur’anic initials were developed and studied in the course of this research. Thus, the correlation between the Qur’anic initials and the names of God mentioned in the initialed sura was studied. Interestingly, some correlation was found, and it was consistent enough to warrant mention here. In sure Al-Baqara, for example, which is prefixed with the initials Alif Lām Mīm (A.L.M.), it was found that the glorious names or attributes of God which terminate certain verses of this sura begin with the letters Alif Lām (A.L.) and end with the letter Mīm (M). These names and their locations in sura Al-Baqara (no. 2) are shown in Table 7.

## Page 46

This relationship was observed as well in sura Āl-'Imrān (no. 3) which is also initialed by A.L.M. Moreover, the subsequent suras which contain no Qur'anic initials did not exhibit the same phenomenon. The set-back for a possible generalization of this hypothesis came when a study of the other initials did not confirm this observation.

A second hypothesis was tested whereby the correlation between the Qur'anic initials and the names of God's messengers or prophets was examined. No consistent correlation was found, and this hypothesis was also discarded. Many other fruitless attempts were made to find some consistent pattern related to the Qur'anic initials and their suras. Finally, the decision was made to physically count all the alphabetic letters which make up each one of the Qur'an's 114 suras. In order to find all the possible mathematical correlations among the letters in general, and the Qur'anic initials in particular, electronic computers had to be deployed.

## Page 47

# Chapter 5

## METHODS OF CALCULATION

The only calculations carried out manually were the initial determinations of the frequency of occurrence of each letter in each sura. A special form was prepared as shown in Figure 1 to facilitate the counting process. As shown in Figure 1, the name of the sura, the number of verses in that sura, its chronological number in the sequence of revelation, and its number in the sequence of recording were listed at the top of the form. The 28 alphabetic letters were listed on the right-hand side of the form on a vertical line. The counting was carried out by reading the sura while placing a short vertical line in front of each letter read. Thus, as

![Arabic text](بِسْمِ اللَّهِ الرَّحْمَٰنِ الرَّحِيمِ)

was read, a vertical mark was placed in front of the letter Bā, then in front of the letter Sīn, then in front of the letter Mīm, then in front of the letter Alif, then two marks were placed in front of the letter Lām, and so forth. Every fifth mark was a slash across the previous four marks. When a sura was completed, the number of each letter was obtained by counting the marks in front of it, and the total number of alphabetic letters was calculated by adding up the numbers of all 28 letters. The final result for sura Al-Fātiḥa (no. 1) is illustrated in Figure 2. All subsequent calculations were carried out by means of a Xerox Data System’s Sigma 7 electronic computer, utilizing a teletype terminal (Time-Share

Figure 2

## Page 50

Network). All programming was done in FORTRAN IV language. A library program of Com-Share, Inc. entitled "MARS" was used for the arrangement of the final data in ascending order.

For each of the 14 letters which participate in the Qur'anic initials, the following three values were determined:

1. **The Absolute Frequency:** This was simply the number of occurrences of each letter in each sura.

2. **The Percentage of Frequency:** This value was calculated by dividing the frequency of occurrence of each letter by the total number of letters in the sura, then multiplying the product by 100.

3. **The Average Per Verse Value:** This was determined by dividing the absolute frequency of a given letter by the number of verses which constitute the sura.

For a multi-lettered set of Qur'anic initials, these three values were determined by adding up the corresponding values of all the constituent letters. Thus, the absolute frequency values were added together, the percentage values were added together, and the average per verse values were added together. To illustrate, let us consider sura Al-Baqara. This sura is made up of 286 verses, with the first verse consisting of three alphabetic letters, Alif Lām Mīm (A.L.M.). The total number of letters in this sura is 25,717. The absolute frequency of occurrence of the letter Alif (A) is 4,329. This makes the percentage value of the letter Alif (A) in sura Al-Baqara = (4,329/25,717) x 100 = 16.8%. The average per verse

## Page 51

occurrence of the letter Alif (A) in sura Al-Baqara would be 4,329/286 = 15.14. The corresponding values for the letter Lām (L) are 2,941, 11.4%, and 10.28. For the letter Mīm (M), the three values were determined as 2,195, 8.5%, and 7.67, respectively. Thus, the absolute frequency value for the set of initials Alif Lām Mīm (A.L.M.) was determined by adding 4,329 + 2,941 + 2,195 = 9,465. Similarly, the percentage value of ALM in sura Al-Baqara was determined by adding 16.8 + 11.4 + 8.5 = 36.7%. The average per verse value of ALM in Al-Baqara was calculated as 15.14 + 10.28 + 7.67 = 33.09. Of course, all these computations were carried out by the computer. The three values for each one of the Qur’anic initials in each one of the 114 suras were thus determined.

The reason for following this particular method for calculation of the multi-lettered Qur’anic initials is the assertion by Prophet Muhammad (peace be upon him) that their constituent letters are independent of each other. This was in a ḥadīth reported by Al-Bukhārī, Al-Bayḍawī, and Al-Suyūṭy. The Prophet said, “I do not say that Alif Lām Mīm (A.L.M.) constitutes one entity. I say that Alif (A) is an independent letter, Lām (L) is an independent letter, and Mīm (M) is an independent letter.”

The following data were fed into the computer:

1. The absolute frequency of occurrence of each letter which participates in Qur’anic initials, in each sura. These letters are Alif (A), Hā (H), Rā (R), Sīn (S),

## Page 52

Sād (Ṣ), Ṭā (Ṭ), ‘Ayn (‘A), Qāf (Q), Kāf (K), Lām (L), Mīm (M), Nūn (N), Hā (H), and Yā (Y). The computer print-out of these values is shown in Table 8.

## Page 54

2. The total number of letters in each sura.
3. The number of verses in each sura.

The computer was programmed to perform the following functions:

1. Compute the percentage value for each one of the 14 letters in each of the 114 suras.
2. Compute the average frequency of occurrence of each one of the 14 letters in each verse of each sura.
3. Compute the absolute frequency of occurrence of the multi-lettered Qur’anic initials, namely Ṭā Hā (Ṭ.H.), Ṭā Sīn (Ṭ.S.), Yā Sīn (Y.S.), Ḥā Mīm (Ḥ.M.), Alif Lām Mīm (A.L.M.), Alif Lām Rā (A.L.R.), Ṭā Sīn Mīm (Ṭ.S.M.), Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād (A.L.M.Ṣ.), Alif Lām Mīm Rā (A.L.M.R.), Kāf Hā Yā ‘Ayn Ṣād (K.H.Y.‘A.Ṣ.), and Ḥā Mīm-‘Ayn Sīn Qāf (Ḥ.M.‘A.S.Q.). Table 9 is a computer printout listing the absolute frequency of occurrence of the Qur’anic initials in each sura.
4. Compute the percentage of frequency value for each of the multi-lettered initials in each of the 114 suras. Table 10 is a computer printout of the percentage frequency values of all the Qur’anic initials throughout the Qur’an.
5. Compute the average occurrence per verse for each of the multi-lettered Qur’anic initials in each sura. These data are presented in Table 11 which is a computer printout of the average per verse values for each of 14 Qur’anic initials in each one of the Qur’an’s 114 suras.

## Page 55

6. Arrange all the suras of Qur’an in ascending order with regard to the absolute frequency of occurrence of each Qur’anic initial. Thus, for each one of the Qur’anic initials, the sura which contains the lowest frequency of occurrence appears at the top of the list, and the sura containing the highest frequency appears at the bottom of the list. It was initially intended to have the computer list the suras in a “descending” order, to have the sura with the highest value at the top, rather than the bottom of the list. However, due to a programming error, this particular command to the computer became reversed. This of course does not affect the results or the conclusions in the least.

7. Arrange the 114 suras of the Qur’an in ascending order with regard to the percent frequency of occurrence for each of the 14 sets of Qur’anic initials.

8. Arrange all suras of the Qur’an in ascending order with regard to the average occurrence in each verse of each sura. The computer printouts of all the lists are shown within the text, and will be referred to as required.

In searching for a single value which will represent each one of the Qur’anic initials most accurately, the percentage value stands out as the best possible choice. Certainly, we cannot use the absolute frequency of occurrence of a given letter, or a set of letters, as a representative measure or as a basis for comparing the various suras. For example, we cannot compare a long sura such as Al-Baqara (286 verses, 25,717 alphabetic

## Page 56

letters), with a short sura such as Al-Nās (3 verses, 99 alphabetic letters) on the basis of absolute frequency of occurrence. Similarly, the average per verse value cannot be used as a truly representative measure of the frequency of occurrence of the Qur’anic initials. The percentage value, therefore, appears to be the only value which can be used as a basis for comparison. However, we will have to take into consideration that the percentage value does not ALWAYS offer a good representation of the frequency value. It can be distorted by such factors as the lack of sufficient component units (statistically known as observations). To illustrate this point, let us take the extreme example of a chapter which consists of only the two letters A and B. The percent of each of these letters would be 50%, an extraordinarily high percentage value. If we compare the letter A in each chapter of the book, the hypothetical two-lettered chapter undoubtedly will have the highest percentage value (50%). But we cannot honestly conclude from this comparison that this two-lettered chapter “has the highest frequency of the letter A.” In this study, we will come across instances where the percentage value is obviously distorted by the extreme shortness of certain suras. Whenever the percentage value is questionable (as a true representative of the frequency), we will have to consider carefully all three values of the Qur’anic initial under study (the absolute number, the percentage, and the average per verse value). These instances and the computations involved in them will be discussed in detail wherever the occasion arises in the text.

## Page 57

Additionally, since different letters make up different sets of Qur’anic initials, we do not expect all the initials to exhibit one and the same phenomenon (e.g. the frequency of occurrence). Thus all three values may be needed under certain circumstances as we study the Qur’anic initials.

## Page 74

Chapter 6

RESULTS, OBSERVATIONS, AND DISCUSSION

1. THE QUR’ANIC INITIAL QĀF (Q):

This initial occurs in two suras. One sura, namely, Qāf (Q), bears the same initial as its title. It is sura number 50 in the recorded sequence of Qur’an; number 34 in the chronological sequence of revelation (see Tables 2 and 3). The other sura is Al-Shūrā (Consensus) which is initialed with two different sets of letters; the first verse is made up of the Qur’anic initials Hā Mīm (H.M.), and the second verse consists of the letters ‘Ayn Sīn Qāf (‘A.S.Q.). It is sura number 42 in the recording sequence, and ranks 62 in the chronological sequence of revelation.

Table 12 is a computer printout of the percentage values of the letter Qāf (Q) in each one of the Qur’an’s 114 suras. The suras are arranged in ascending order with regard to the percent frequency of the letter Qāf. For the reader’s convenience, the titles of suras have been added onto the computer lists.

The reader will note that sura Qāf (Q) occupies position number 111 out of the 114 listings. This means that sura Qāf (Q) contains the highest percentage of the letter Qāf (Q) than any other sura in the Qur’an, with the exception of three suras. The only suras which contain a higher percentage of Qāf (Q) are Al-Shams (The Sun), Al-Qiyāma (Resurrection), and Al-Falaq (Daybreak).

## Page 81

Careful examination of these three suras reveals that they are extremely short. This fact raises the possibility that the percentage value of Qāf might be distorted out of true representation of frequency due to the extreme shortness of these suras. This type of statistical distortion was explained on page 40. Furthermore, the careful examination reveals that all three suras are vastly inferior to sura Qāf in the overall frequency value of the letter Qāf (Q). Thus, both the absolute frequency of Qāf (Q), and the average per verse value are lower in these three suras than in sura Qāf. Table 13 is a computer printout of the absolute frequency values of Qāf, and Table 14 is a computer printout of the average per verse values of Qāf throughout the Qur’an. Table 15 shows the detailed determination of the overall frequency of the letter Qāf in sura Qāf, and in the three short suras exhibiting a higher percentage of Qāf. We note from Tables 13 and 15 that sura Al-Shams (The Sun) contains a total of 10 Qāfs compared to 57 in sura Qāf. Similarly, we note that sura Al-Qiyāma (Resurrection) contains 27 Qāfs (Q’s), and sura Al-Falaq (Daybreak) contains but 6 Qāfs.

Computation of the Overall Frequency Values of the Qur’anic Initial Qāf (Q) in Sura Qāf and the Three Suras Which Contain a Higher Percentage of Qāf(Q).

| Sura       | Absolute Number | Difference | Percent Value | Difference | Avg./Verse | Difference | Net  |
|------------|------------------|------------|----------------|------------|------------|------------|------|
| Qāf        | 57               | 0          | 3.782          | 0          | 1.267      | 0          | 0    |
| Al-Shams   | 10               | -4.700     | 3.906          | +0.033     | 0.667      | -0.899     | -5.566|
| Al-Qiyāma  | 27               | -1.100     | 3.907          | +0.033     | 0.675      | -0.877     | -1.944|

## Page 82

From Tables 14 and 15 we note that the average per verse value of the letter Qāf in sura Qāf is 1.267. The corresponding value in sura Al-Shams is 0.667; in sura Al-Qiyāma it is 0.675, and in sura Al-Falaq it is 1.200. The net OVERALL frequency, derived from all three values of the letter Qāf in the four suras, is presented in Table 15. The computation was carried out by determining the magnitude of difference above and/or below sura Qāf with regard to each one of the three values, in each sura. The magnitude of difference was computed by deducting the smaller value from the larger value, then dividing the difference by the smaller value. A plus sign would indicate that the difference is above the reference sura (sura Qāf in this case), and a minus sign would indicate that the difference is below the reference sura. To illustrate, let us compare sura A which contains 75 occurrences of a given letter with sura B which contains 25 occurrences of the same letter. The magnitude of difference in this case equals (75-25)/25 = 2.000. If sura A were the reference sura, a plus sign should precede the magnitude of difference (+2.000). This means that the absolute frequency of the letter in question is higher in sura A than in sura B by a two-fold magnitude. If sura B were the reference sura, a minus sign would precede the 2.000.

By comparing the net overall frequency of the letter Qāf in sura Qāf with the overall frequency in the three suras exhibiting a higher percentage value, we note that sura Qāf is actually more than 5 times greater than

## Page 83

sura Al-Shams, almost two times greater than sura Al-Qiyāma, and more than 7 times greater than sura Al-Falaq (see Table 15). These observations lead us to the conclusion that the only three suras in the Qur’an which exhibit a higher percentage of Qāf than that in sura Qāf are in fact inferior to sura Qāf in the overall frequency. This means that **SURA QĀF CONTAINS THE HIGHEST FREQUENCY OF THE LETTER QĀF THAN ANY OTHER SURA IN THE QUR’AN.**

What does this startling observation mean? For one thing, it means that someone knew that a particular sura will have the highest overall frequency value of the letter Qāf. To indicate this knowledge, this particular sura was “tagged” with the letter Qāf, and also entitled “Qāf.”

The next logical question would be, “Who is that ‘SOMEONE’?” Who could possibly acquire *advance knowledge* about the distribution of the alphabet throughout the Qur’an? As we ask these questions, we should bear in mind that the Qur’an was revealed over a period of 23 years, and that the verses within each sura were often widely separated both in time and place. It takes little thinking to realize that ONLY GOD ALMIGHTY can acquire such knowledge. Perhaps this explains to us the wisdom behind revealing the Qur’an in this particular fashion, coupled with deliberate concealment of the significance of the Qur’anic initials. This point will be explained in detail in the Epilogue section of this book. Within sura Qāf, one verse, namely, verse number 38, was revealed in Medina, more than 300 miles

## Page 84

and many years away from other verses of this Meccan sura. Yet, this one verse contains two Qāfs, a higher than average occurrence (as we know, the average per verse value of the letter Qāf in sura Qāf is 1.267).

The only alternative to this conclusion would identify that "SOMEONE" as the Prophet Muhammad (peace be upon him). This alternative conclusion, however, will have to assume that the Prophet, an illiterate man, had an advance knowledge of the distribution pattern of the letter Qāf and its exact proportions throughout 114 chapters to be revealed in scattered verses over the next 23 years. The reader can easily see the farfetchedness of such a conclusion. And we are yet talking about only one, the first, of 14 Qur’anic initials. Even without going any further in this study, we already have sufficient evidence that ONLY GOD COULD BE THE AUTHOR OF QUR’AN.

Lest someone may actually claim that the mathematical superiority of sura Qāf in the overall frequency value of the letter Qāf is coincidental, God Almighty provided further evidence supporting the observed superiority. Thus, the only other sura in the Qur’an which bears the letter Qāf as a Qur’anic initial, namely, sura Al-Shūrā (Consensus; No. 42), contains 57 Qāfs. This is the same frequency of occurrence found in sura Qāf (see Table 13). Furthermore, when we add the 57 Qāfs of sura Qāf plus the 57 Qāfs of sura Al-Shūrā, the product is 114, same as the total number of suras in the Qur’an. If the letter “Q” stands for “Qur’an,” we have in these

## Page 85

two Qāf-initialized suras one Qāf for each sura. The clear message here is that all 114 suras constitute the Qur’an, the whole Qur’an, and nothing but the Qur’an.

The importance of this observation, and all the other observations in this study, is the fact that anyone can SEE them for himself. No guesswork or speculation is involved. Thus, anyone anywhere in the world can pick up a copy of the Qur’an, and count the letter Qāf in sura Qāf and in sura Al-Shūrā, the only suras initialized with the letter Qāf. This process which takes no more than 30 minutes would unveil the physical, factual, observation that these two suras contain the same number of Qāfs (Q’s), 57. In just 30 minutes, anyone in the world can obtain for himself this observation which is no less significant than the previous observation concerning overall mathematical superiority. In just 30 minutes, anyone in the world can SEE FOR HIMSELF physical evidence that the Qur’an is God’s unaltered word. In these two Qāf-initialized suras, the presence of one more word (or one less) such as Qul (say), or Huq (truth), or Qawm (folk), or any substitutions or alterations thereof during the past 14 centuries would have nullified this significant observation.

## Page 86

2. **THE QUR’ANIC INITIAL Nūn (N):**

This initial occurs in one sura entitled Al-Qalam (The Pen). It is sura number 68 in the recorded sequence of suras; number 2 in the chronological sequence of revelation. Tables 16, 17, and 18 are the computer printouts of the percentage values of Nūn (N), the absolute frequency of occurrence values, and the average per verse values in all suras, respectively. The suras are arranged in ascending order with regard to these values. By looking at the list of percentage values of Nūn (Table 16), we note that sura Al-Qalam has a very high percentage of Nūn (N). It occupies position number 109 out of the 114 listings. This means that sura Al-Qalam (The Pen), which is initialed with the letter Nūn, has the highest percentage of Nūn, with the exception of only 5 suras. However, careful examination of these five suras reveals that three of them are suras which contain Qur’anic initials. These three suras are Al-Shu‘arā’ (Poets), Al-Dukhān (Air Pollution), and Al-Hijr (The Hijr Valley). The Qur’anic initials which prefix these suras are Ṭā Sīn Mīm (Ṭ.S.M.), Ḥā Mīm (Ḥ.M.), and Alif Lām Rā (A.L.R.), respectively. Moreover, we note that all three suras belong to “groups” of suras which bear identical Qur’anic initials. Thus, sura Al-Shu‘arā’ belongs to the Ṭā Sīn Mīm group which includes sura Al-Qaṣaṣ (History). Sura Al-Dukhān belongs to the Ḥā Mīm group which includes six other sister suras (see Tables 4, 5, and 6). Sura Al-Ḥijr has 4 sister suras which bear the initials Alif Lām Rā. As this study progresses, we will

## Page 87

find that the suras with identical initials must be treated as one unit, as if they were one continuous sura. When we combine the three suras under study here with their sister suras, we note that the average percentage of the letter Nūn in the unified groups is indeed inferior to the percentage contained in sura Al-Qalam. These averaging computations are shown in Table 19.

We already note that the mathematical superiority of sura Al-Qalam with regard to its initial (Nūn), is not as strong as that of sura Qāf with regard to the letter Qāf. We also note that while sura Qāf is entitled “Qāf” in addition to having Qāf as a Qur’anic initial, this is not the case with sura Al-Qalam. This Nūn-initialized sura is not entitled “Nūn.” One expects the different Qur’anic initials to exhibit different characteristics. Thus, we find the requirement for averaging the suras with identical initials in the case of Nūn (N). As the reader recalls, no such averaging was required to see the superiority of the Qur’anic initial Qāf. It is through this diversity, and precision, that the miracle of the Qur’an becomes manifest as this study proceeds. Also, this phenomenon provides us with a possible answer to the question, “Why do certain suras bear identical initials?”

After satisfying the requirement for averaging percent Nūn (N) in the sister suras which contain the initials Ṭā Sīn Mīm, Ḥā Mīm, and Alif Lām Rā, we note that only two suras in the whole Qur’an contain a higher percentage of Nūn than sura Al-Qalam. These two suras are Al-Sāffāt (The Lineup), and Al-Wāqi‘a (The Inev-

## Page 88

itable). Yet, by studying Table 18, we note that both suras are inferior to sura Al-Qalam in the average per verse occurrence of the letter Nūn. While sura Al-Qalam contains on the average 2.558 Nūns (N’s) in each verse, sura Al-Ṣaffāt contains an average 2.385 Nūns per verse, and sura Al-Wāqiʻa contains an average 2.014 Nūns in each verse. Thus, the mathematical superiority of the Nūn-initialized sura (Al-Qalam) with regard to the frequency of the letter Nūn is unquestionable. After satisfying the requirement for averaging the percentage value of Nūn in the suras with identical initials, we find that **THERE IS NOT ONE SURA IN THE WHOLE QUR’AN WHERE ALL THREE VALUES OF NŪN ARE HIGHER THAN THEIR COUNTERPARTS IN THE NŪN-INITIALED SURA, AL-QALAM.**

## Page 96

3. THE QUR’ANIC INITIAL Ṣād (Ṣ):

This initial occurs in two suras which were revealed consecutively, but separated in the recording sequence. The first sura is entitled “Ṣād (Ṣ),” and it ranks 38 both in the revelation order and recording order. The second sura is Al-A‘rāf (The Purgatory) which ranks 39 in the revelation sequence, and 7 in the order of recording. Sura Ṣād has the letter Ṣād as the only initial, while sura Al-A‘rāf bears three other letters as Qur’anic initials, namely, Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād (A.L.M.Ṣ.). The computer printouts of the percentage values of the letter Ṣād, its absolute frequency of occurrence values, and its average per verse values, are shown in Tables 20, 21, and 22, respectively. By examining the list of percentage values, the reader will note that sura Ṣād ranks 88 out of the 114 suras. Further examination reveals that the suras which exhibit a higher percentage of Ṣād than sura Ṣād are all, without exception, very short suras. This fact is illustrated in Table 23 which lists these suras and their content of the letter Ṣād. For sure, by studying the three computer listings (Tables 20, 21, and 22), we note that **THERE IS NOT ONE SURA IN THE WHOLE QUR’AN WHERE ALL THREE VALUES OF THE LETTER ṢĀD ARE HIGHER THAN THEIR COUNTERPARTS IN SURA ṢĀD.**

As previously illustrated on page 40, the extreme shortness of these suras may contribute to a distortion of their percentage values beyond true representation of the frequency. This warrants a detailed look at the

## Page 97

different values of Ṣād in each one of these short suras. The overall frequency value of the letter Ṣād and the net magnitudes of difference in relation to sura Ṣād have been computed as explained on page 68. The results are shown in Table 24. This table shows that there are three suras which carry a plus sign, i.e., they are superior to sura Ṣād in the net overall frequency of the letter Ṣād. Therefore, although sura Ṣād contains a very high relative frequency of the letter Ṣād, its superiority is not absolute. It has the highest overall frequency of the letter Ṣād than any other sura in the Qur’an, with the exception of the three suras indicated in Table 24, namely, Al-Hashr (The Thronging), Al-Mulk (Kingship), and Al-'Asr (The Afternoon). These data remind us that the letter Ṣād, as a Qur’anic initial, covers not only sura Ṣād, but also sura Al-A‘rāf (The Purgatory) which is a continuation of sura Ṣād as far as the letter Ṣād is concerned (chronologically, these two suras rank 38 and 39. See Tables 3 and 6). The percentage of the letter Ṣād is 0.954 in sura Ṣād, and 0.688 in sura Al-A‘rāf. The average of these two values is 0.821%. Using this average, all suras with a higher than 0.821 percentage of Ṣād were studied in detail as shown in Table 25. All three values of Ṣād in these suras were considered, and the magnitudes of difference above and below the corresponding average of (sura Ṣād + sura Al-A‘rāf) were computed as explained previously. As it turned out, only one sura, Yūsuf (Joseph), exhibited a plus sign for the net overall frequency of Ṣād (see Table 25). But sura

## Page 98

Yūsuf itself is an initialed sura which belongs to the Alif Lām Rā group (see table 4). When this sura is considered with its sister suras, we find that the average percentage of Ṣād is indeed lower than in sura Ṣād or sura Al-A‘rāf (Table 26). Thus, by averaging the percentage value of Ṣād in the five A.L.R.-suras, we observe that the letter Ṣād has its highest relative frequency in the united suras Ṣād/Al-A‘rāf which are initialed with the letter Ṣād.

This observation teaches us that the existence of the letter Ṣād as a Qur’anic initial in sura Al-A‘rāf is no accident. WE LEARN THAT EVERY SINGLE LETTER IN THE ENTIRE QUR’AN WAS CAREFULLY MEASURED AND CALCULATED. As we saw from these data, sura Ṣād by itself did not exhibit absolute superiority with regard to the initial Ṣād. We were unable to ignore the continuation of the Qur’anic initial Ṣād into the subsequent sura entitled Al-A‘rāf. Since sura Al-A‘rāf contains three other initials besides Ṣād, namely, Alif (A), Lām (L), and Mīm (M), a detailed discussion of this sura will follow later.

## Page 109

4. **THE QUR’ANIC INITIALS Ṭā Hā (Ṭ.H.):**

This set of initials occurs in one sura whose title consists of the same two letters. Sura Ṭā Hā is the twentieth chapter in the recording order of Qur’an, while its chronological order of revelation is 45. The percentage occurrence of Ṭā Hā, the absolute frequency of occurrence, and the average occurrence per verse values are shown in Tables 27, 28, and 29, respectively. We note from the percentage list that sura Ṭā Hā clearly has a very high relative frequency of the letters Ṭā Hā. To find out what special characteristics sura Ṭā Hā might have with regard to its Qur’anic initials, a closer look at the suras with higher percentages of Ṭā Hā is warranted. Computation of the overall frequency value of Ṭā Hā was carried out for these suras, and the results are shown in Table 30. We note from this table that five suras are superior to sura Ṭā Hā in the overall frequency value of the letters Ṭā Hā. These five suras are Al-Ḥajj (Pilgrimage), Al-Nūr (The Light), Al-Fatḥ (Victory), Al-Mujādala (The Argument), and Al-Tawba (Repentance). But we also note that all these five are Medinan suras. No Meccan sura is superior to sura Ṭā Hā in the overall frequency of the letters Ṭā Hā. For sure, by examining the three computer listings in Tables 27, 28, and 29, we find that there is not one Meccan sura in the whole Qur’an where all three values of Ṭā Hā are higher than their counterparts in sura Ṭā Hā. From these data, therefore, we observe that **SURA ṬĀ HĀ IS SUPERIOR TO ALL MECCAN SURAS** with

## Page 110

regard to the Qur'anic initials **Ṭā Hā**. The reader is reminded that sura **Ṭā Hā** is a Meccan sura. Together with later data in this study, this set of initials serves to confirm for us the location of revelation of the various suras. This conclusion will be discussed in detail elsewhere.

We should also note that the letter **Hā** occurs as an initial in sura Maryam which *precedes* sura **Ṭā Hā**, both in the recording sequence and the chronological sequence. However, combining the values of **Hā** in these two suras does not impart any changes on the final results or conclusions.

## Page 118

5. **THE QUR’ANIC INITIALS Yā Sīn (Y.S.):**

This set of initials occurs in one sura whose title is also Yā Sīn (Y.S.). Sura Yā Sīn ranks 36 in the recorded sequence of the Qur’an; 41 in the chronological sequence of revelation. The percentage values of Yā Sīn, the absolute frequency values, and the average per verse values, throughout the Qur’an, are shown in Tables 31, 32, and 33, respectively. Although this book may not make use of certain data and/or tables presented here, the data are provided in hopes that many other people will use them eventually to discover new facts relating to the miraculous composition of the Qur’an. Undoubtedly, this book only scratches the surface; merely uncovers the tip of a huge iceberg. Scholars who are much more versed in mathematics than this author may reach better methods of computation and/or value representation. Therefore, all the raw data are included in this book.

When we look at Table 31, we note right away that sura Yā Sīn has an extremely low relative frequency of the two letters Yā Sīn (Y.S.). The only suras in the whole Qur’an which contain a lower percentage of Yā Sīn are five extremely short suras. These five suras are Al-‘Aṣr (The Afternoon), Al-Falaq (Daybreak), Al-Kawthar (Bounty), Al-Shams (The Sun), and Al-Kāfirūn (The Disbelievers) (see Table 31). The total number of alphabetic letters in these five suras are 90, 90, 61, 256, and 114, respectively. As illustrated on page 40, this extreme shortness leads to distortion of the percentage

## Page 119

value as a statistically representative value for the frequency of occurrence.

It appears, therefore, that the special significance of sura Yā Sīn is that it has the lowest relative frequency of the letters Yā Sīn. This is of course a complete reversal of the observations made so far about the Qur’anic initials. It may be relevant here to note that the initials Yā Sīn themselves represent a reversal in the alphabetical sequence. The first letter in this set of Qur’anic initials, Yā, is the LAST letter of the Arabic alphabet (see Table 1). The letter Sīn, on the other hand, is a relatively early letter in the alphabetical sequence. When we survey the other Qur’anic initials, we find that the first two initials usually consist of an early letter in the common sequence of the Arabic alphabet, followed by a late letter. Thus, we observe this pattern in the Qur’anic initials Ṭā→Hā, Ḥā→Mīm, Alif→Lām Mīm, Alif→Lām Rā, Alif→Lām Mīm Ṣād, Alif→Lām Mīm Rā, Kāf→Ḥā Yā ‘Ayn Ṣād, and Ḥā→Mīm ‘Ayn Sīn Qāf. Only Yā←Sīn, and Ṭā←Sīn represent a reversal of this pattern. We have seen in this section that the mathematical superiority phenomenon is reversed as far as the initials Yā Sīn in sura Yā Sīn are concerned. We shall see later that the initials Ṭā Sīn and Ṭā Sīn Mīm also exhibit unique mathematical phenomena.

It should be pointed out, however, that although the common arrangement for the Arabic alphabet is the one shown in Table 1, as well as in Webster’s New Collegiate Dictionary, there have been different arrangements

## Page 120

during the ancient, pre-Qur’anic times. The observation concerning alphabet reversals in Yā Sīn, therefore, may be challenged by certain critics. In any case, this particular observation, or lack of it, has no effect whatsoever on the mathematical properties of sura Yā Sīn.
